# Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies

Haoxuan Wang Affiliation: Department of Statistical Science, Duke
University, Durham, NC, 27708, U.S.A.    Patrik Lauha Affiliation:
Organismal and Evolutionary Biology Research Programme, Faculty of
Biological and Environmental Sciences, University of Helsinki, Helsinki,
00014, Finland. Affiliation: Department of Biological and Environmental
Science, Faculty of Mathematics and Science, University of Jyväskylä,
Jyväskylä, 40014, Finland    David B. Dunson Affiliation: Department of
Statistical Science, Duke University, Durham, NC, 27708, U.S.A.

###### Abstract

1\. Ecological and conservation studies rely on reliable species
observations. In the case of birds, such data are typically produced
through acoustic monitoring. Recently, machine learning algorithms have
emerged that can accurately classify bird species from audio recordings,
but such algorithms crucially depend on expert-labeled training data.
Automated classification is particularly challenging when multiple
species vocalize simultaneously, recordings contain background noise, or
birds are far from the microphone. Passive acoustic monitoring generates
massive audio datasets, yet human experts can label only a tiny
proportion of the available data, and experts may differ in their
accuracy and breadth of knowledge across species.

2\. We focus on combining sparse expert annotations to improve the
quality of labels while providing uncertainty quantification. We propose
a Bayesian hierarchical modeling framework tailored to multi-species
identification that includes a family of model variants, aggregates
sparse annotations, accounts for correlation among species
vocalizations, and models heterogeneity in expert performance across
species through an additional hierarchical structure. The framework
produces posterior probabilities of species presence together with
uncertainty measures and expert performance scores intended to support
feedback, engagement, and improvement.

3\. In comprehensive simulation studies, the proposed model variants
outperform majority vote and simpler alternatives, especially in sparse
and highly correlated annotation settings. We further evaluate the
approach using data from a community science platform developed in
Finland, comprising 3,997 audio clips with extremely sparse labels
produced by 46 annotators. Using a subset of recordings annotated by a
highly reliable ornithologist as a gold standard, our framework achieves
substantially higher accuracy than majority vote and provides
better-calibrated uncertainty estimates.

4\. These results demonstrate that combining sparse expert annotations
within our framework can substantially improve the quality of labels for
ecological datasets. The framework offers a practical pathway for
integrating limited expert effort into long-term ecological monitoring
and conservation workflows, and enables scalable and uncertainty-aware
analysis of the annotations. Even though we demonstrate our framework
with labels for bird audio, the approach is applicable to any
multi-label ecological data annotated by several experts or citizen
scientists.

Corresponding author: Haoxuan Wang, H. Milton Stewart School of
Industrial and Systems Engineering, Georgia Institute of Technology, 755
Ferst Drive NW, Atlanta, GA 30332-0205, U.S.A. Email:
[hwang3111@gatech.edu](mailto:hwang3111@gatech.edu)

Keywords: Annotation; Bayesian modeling; Bio-monitoring; Crowd sourcing;
Ecology; Measurement error; Multiple annotators

## 1 Introduction

Bio-monitoring is undergoing a remarkable technological revolution,
driven by autonomous data collection devices, including passive acoustic
recording units and camera traps. These innovations have ushered in an
era of cost-effective, large-scale data acquisition, which has proven
invaluable for ecological research ([Shonfield and Bayne,
2017](#bib.bib36)), motivating development of deep learning based
species identification ([Kahl et al., 2021](#bib.bib31)). However,
creating accurate and reliable annotations for the massive amount of
noisy unlabeled data that are now being routinely collected is a
daunting challenge. We are motivated by the problem of identifying which
bird species are present at a location based on audio recordings. Deep
neural networks are promising, but require large training datasets
consisting of audio recordings of known bird species ([Kahl et al.,
2021](#bib.bib31)). Although such data are available in online
libraries, these are typically weakly labelled and might not match the
data from bio-monitoring programs in terms of recording quality. It is
critical to have strongly labeled training data under realistic
conditions in the field for the region of interest to train a species
classifier with sufficient accuracy ([Lauha et al., 2022](#bib.bib32)).
Unfortunately, annotation of recordings requires expert knowledge and is
a laborious task ([Lehikoinen et al., 2023](#bib.bib30)).

In response to this challenge, crowdsourcing has emerged as a popular
and widely adopted technique for annotating large-scale datasets across
various disciplines ([Swanson et al., 2016](#bib.bib35)). Crowdsourcing
platforms such as MTurk provide task requesters with an online
marketplace to post a batch of microtasks for some workers to complete
for a small monetary compensation ([Yin et al., 2021](#bib.bib6)).
Likewise, citizen science has become more and more popular among
scientists, and shows great potential for accomplishing large-scale
tasks by sharing the workload ([Franzoni et al., 2022](#bib.bib33)).
Numerous bird hobbyists with extensive experience in identifying bird
vocalizations are an ideal audience for crowdsourcing ornithological
data ([Sullivan et al., 2009](#bib.bib37); [Lehikoinen et al.,
2023](#bib.bib30)).

Our motivation is drawn from the Finnish Kerttu web portal ([Lehikoinen
et al., 2023](#bib.bib30)), which is a crowdsourcing project leveraging
on birdwatcher expertise in annotating the species of birds vocalizing
in audio recordings. The portal was designed to enable annotation of
audio recordings collected with autonomous recorders. While the portal
has subsequently been upgraded to cover global soundscapes
([https://bsg.laji.fi/identification/instructions](https://bsg.laji.fi/identification/instructions)),
the initial focus was on ten locations in Southern Finland. The users
were asked to list all bird species vocalizing in the recording and to
indicate if there are other bird species that they are not able to
identify. The platform also asked users to rate their own bird sound
identification skills, while providing them with feedback to improve
their abilities and encourage their continued engagement with the site.
In this paper, we use data produced in this project consisting of
$`\mathchar 28723\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28727`$
$`\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720`$-second audio clips, which have been
annotated by one or more bird experts. These data are described and
referred to as "clips" in the paper by [Lehikoinen et al.
(2023)](#bib.bib30).

A critical question in this application, and other settings involving
citizen scientist crowdsourcing, is how to account for the inevitable
errors and variation in accuracy of the annotations provided by
different users ([Aceves-Bueno et al., 2017](#bib.bib34)). Even
individuals with substantial expertise will face challenges in providing
accurate classifications in cases with high noise, rare species,
substantial distance from the recording, short vocalizations, species
having similar vocalizations or multiple species having overlapping
vocalizations. Errors through misclicks can also occur. Hence, in
practice there are commonly inconsistencies in the annotations of
different experts. To reduce error, one can assign each audio recording
to multiple experts and then aggregate these annotations. Most existing
aggregation methods focus on single-label scenarios ([Johnson,
1996](#bib.bib15); [Raykar et al., 2010](#bib.bib8); [Ghosh et al.,
2011](#bib.bib17); [Dalvi et al., 2013](#bib.bib18); [Lin et al.,
2018](#bib.bib16); [Kim et al., 2021](#bib.bib14)). However, in bird
species annotation, we are faced with a multi-label crowdsourcing task.
In this work we propose a solution for multi-label annotation
aggregation. A more detailed description of this motivating application
and the corresponding problem formulation is given in Section
[2.1](#S2.SS1 "2.1 Motivating application and problem formulation ‣ 2 Materials and methods ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies").

In this paper, we introduce a Bayesian hierarchical modeling framework
tailored to the challenges of multi-species identification and apply it
on bird sound annotation data from [Lehikoinen et al.
(2023)](#bib.bib30) (Fig.
[1](#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies")).
We model the joint distribution of the different birds present in an
audio recording in a flexible manner, while accommodating variability in
quality of bird experts’ annotations across different bird species. We
employ informative priors for the model parameters to address the
sparsity of the species annotation data, while using conjugacy and data
augmentation to enable efficient posterior inference through collapsed
Gibbs samplers in the more complex models falling in our framework.

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. Section
[2](#S2 "2 Materials and methods ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies")
presents the motivating application, the problem formulation, and the
components of our Bayesian modeling framework. In Section
[3](#S3 "3 Results ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies"),
we test the performance of several methods within our modeling framework
on simulated data (Section
[3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Simulation results ‣ 3 Results ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies"))
and a dataset of Finnish bird vocalizations which has been annotated by
bird experts through a crowdsourcing project ([Lehikoinen et al.,
2023](#bib.bib30)) (Section
[3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Results for bird species annotations ‣ 3 Results ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies")).
We compare our methods to Majority Vote approach (MV), which was
originally used for annotation aggregation by [Lehikoinen et al.
(2023)](#bib.bib30). Lastly, potential avenues of future research are
discussed in Section
[4](#S4 "4 Discussion ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies").

![Refer to caption](2406.15844v3/workflow_figure.png)

Figure 1: Our framework combines sparse and possibly contradicting
expert annotations into reliable multi-species labels through Bayesian
modeling of expert reliability.

## 2 Materials and methods

In this section, we first describe the motivating bird-sound annotation
problem and introduce the notation used throughout the paper. We then
present several Bayesian hierarchical models for this multi-species
identification task. We begin the modeling part with a straightforward
baseline model in Section
[2.2](#S2.SS2 "2.2 Baseline model ‣ 2 Materials and methods ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies").
Building from this baseline, we propose a nonparametric BMM to induce
dependence in species occurrence in Section
[2.3](#S2.SS3 "2.3 Bernoulli mixture models ‣ 2 Materials and methods ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies")
and
[2.4](#S2.SS4 "2.4 Dirichlet process extension ‣ 2 Materials and methods ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies").
We further extend the measurement error model to account for variation
in annotation performance across species through a hierarchical
structure described in Section
[2.5](#S2.SS5 "2.5 Hierarchical modeling of annotator expertise ‣ 2 Materials and methods ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies").
There are four sub-models falling in our general framework, as
summarized in Table
[1](#S2.T1 "Table 1 ‣ 2 Materials and methods ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies").

| Model               | Species Dependence | Varying Expertise |
| ------------------- | ------------------ | ----------------- |
| Base                | ✗                  | ✗                 |
| Base-Hierarchical   | ✗                  | ✓                 |
| DP-BMM              | ✓                  | ✗                 |
| DP-BMM-Hierarchical | ✓                  | ✓                 |

Table 1: Summary of key features of different models

### 2.1 Motivating application and problem formulation

Our motivating application is bird-sound annotation on the Finnish
Kerttu web portal ([Lehikoinen et al., 2023](#bib.bib30)). Suppose that
$`\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28722}`$ bird experts provide bird-song
identification results for $`\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28721}`$ audio
recordings, and let $`\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28723}`$ denote the
number of candidate bird species. We represent the collected annotations
by a three-dimensional array $`\mathcal{\mathchar 29012}`$, where
$`\mathcal{\mathchar 29012}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721`$
means annotator $`\mathchar 29034`$ reports that species
$`\mathchar 29035`$ is present in recording $`\mathchar 29033`$,
$`\mathcal{\mathchar 29012}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720`$
means annotator $`\mathchar 29034`$ reports that species
$`\mathchar 29035`$ is absent, and
$`\mathcal{\mathchar 29012}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12349\texttt{NA}`$
means that this entry is missing. Missingness is substantial because not
every annotator reviews every recording, and annotators may skip species
when they are not sufficiently confident.

Each recording can contain vocalizations from multiple bird species, so
this is naturally a multi-label annotation aggregation problem ([Bragg
et al., 2013](#bib.bib3); [Li et al., 2016](#bib.bib2); [Li et al.,
2018](#bib.bib4); [Zhang and Wu, 2018](#bib.bib7); [Zhang and Wu,
2019](#bib.bib5); [Shi et al., 2021](#bib.bib1); [Yin et al.,
2021](#bib.bib6)). For recording
$`\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12850\{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28721}\}`$,
let
$`\bm{\mathchar 29049}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28723}}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 29012}`$
denote the latent binary vector indicating which species are truly
present. For annotator
$`\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12850\{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28722}\}`$,
let
$`\mathcal{\mathchar 29011}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 12818\{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12346\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24635\ \mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28723}\}`$
denote the recording–species pairs annotated by annotator
$`\mathchar 29034`$. If
$`\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\hfil\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\delimiter 1299214\hfil$\crcr$\textstyle\mathchar 562$\crcr}}}\mathcal{\mathchar 29011}_{\mathchar 29034}`$,
then
$`\mathcal{\mathchar 29012}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12349\texttt{NA}`$;
otherwise,
$`\mathcal{\mathchar 29012}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12850\{\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721\}`$.
Our first goal is to jointly infer
$`\{\bm{\mathchar 29049}_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\bm{\mathchar 29049}_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\bm{\mathchar 29049}_{\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28721}}\}`$
from the noisy and incomplete annotations $`\mathcal{\mathchar 29012}`$,
while accurately characterizing uncertainty.

A second goal is to model annotator expertise. In binary settings,
annotator models usually rely on either one-coin or two-coin
formulations ([Raykar et al., 2010](#bib.bib8); [Ghosh et al.,
2011](#bib.bib17); [Dalvi et al., 2013](#bib.bib18)). Since sensitivity
and specificity can differ substantially in bird-sound identification,
we use a two-coin formulation and model annotator-specific true positive
rates
$`\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 12349\Pr\delimiter 67273472\mathcal{\mathchar 29012}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785`$
and false positive rates
$`\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 12349\Pr\delimiter 67273472\mathcal{\mathchar 29012}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720\delimiter 84054785`$
for
$`\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 29011}_{\mathchar 29034}`$.
It is also unrealistic to assume that annotators have the same expertise
across all species, which motivates the hierarchical extension
introduced later in this section ([Bragg et al., 2013](#bib.bib3);
[Padmanabhan et al., 2016](#bib.bib9); [Yin et al., 2021](#bib.bib6)).

The motivating dataset is extremely sparse. On average, each bird expert
annotates about 129 recordings, whereas the dataset contains more than
3900 recordings in total, leading to a missing rate of
$`\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28727\mathchar 314\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28726\%`$
in $`\mathcal{\mathchar 29012}`$. At the same time, we have useful prior
knowledge about annotator performance and species occurrence in the
study region. For example, the average agreement score is approximately
$`\mathchar 28728\mathchar 28725\%`$ for species identified by at least
one annotator, and false positive rates are expected to be very low
([Lehikoinen et al., 2023](#bib.bib30)). These features motivate our use
of informative priors for the model parameters.

### 2.2 Baseline model

We start with introducing a simple baseline model corresponding to row 1
of Table
[1](#S2.T1 "Table 1 ‣ 2 Materials and methods ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies").
The latent binary species occurrence data are assumed to follow:

|     |                                                                                                                                                                                                       |     |     |
| --- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12824\text{Bernoulli}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891`$ |     | (1) |

where $`\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29035}`$ represents the occurrence
probability of species $`\mathchar 29035`$ in a random audio segment.
Then, we allow each annotator to have their own TPR
$`\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034}`$ and FPR
$`\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29034}`$ as follows:

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
\text{Pr}\delimiter 67273472\mathcal{\mathchar 29012}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891\qquad\text{Pr}\delimiter 67273472\mathcal{\mathchar 29012}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891
``` |  | (2) |

defined for
$`\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 29011}_{\mathchar 29034}`$.
Equation
([2](#S2.E2 "In 2.2 Baseline model ‣ 2 Materials and methods ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies"))
implies:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathcal{\mathchar 29012}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 12824\text{Bernoulli}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034}^{\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}}\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29034}^{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\ \mathchar 29034\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 314`$ |  | (3) |

Introducing conjugate priors for parameters
$`\bm{\mathchar 29039}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28723}}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 29012}`$,
$`\bm{\mathchar 28949}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28722}}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 29012}`$
and
$`\bm{\mathchar 28960}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28722}}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 29012}`$,
we let

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12824\text{Beta}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 29039}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29026_{\mathchar 29039}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\qquad\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 12824\text{Beta}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 28949}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29026_{\mathchar 28949}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\qquad\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 12824\text{Beta}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 28960}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29026_{\mathchar 28960}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314
``` |  | (4) |

Here $`\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 29039}`$ and
$`\mathchar 29026_{\mathchar 29039}`$ characterize variation in
abundance across different bird species in the region where recordings
are collected. In addition, $`\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 28949}`$ and
$`\mathchar 29026_{\mathchar 28949}`$ characterize the distribution of
TPRs across annotators, while $`\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 28960}`$ and
$`\mathchar 29026_{\mathchar 28960}`$ characterize the distribution of
FPRs. We provide a detailed discussion on choosing appropriate values
for the hyperparameters in Section
[3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Results for bird species annotations ‣ 3 Results ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies").

### 2.3 Bernoulli mixture models

Next, we account for correlations among different bird species by
modeling the distribution of
$`\bm{\mathchar 29049}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28723}}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 29012}`$
as a mixture of Bernoulli distributions ([Bishop, 2006](#bib.bib12)). In
Section
[2.4](#S2.SS4 "2.4 Dirichlet process extension ‣ 2 Materials and methods ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies"),
we introduce a Bayesian nonparametric extension to infer the number of
mixture components $`\mathchar 29010`$. As we add additional components,
the model can accurately characterize arbitrarily complex joint
distributions of the species occurrence indicators ([Dunson and Xing,
2009](#bib.bib40)). For now, assume $`\mathchar 29010`$ is
pre-specified. The joint distribution of
$`\bm{\mathchar 29049}_{\mathchar 29033}`$ is:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathchar 29040\delimiter 67273472\bm{\mathchar 29049}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12906\bm{\mathchar 28953}\mathchar 24891\bm{\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29010}\mathchar 28953_{\mathchar 29042}\mathchar 4945\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28723}}\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}^{\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}^{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891`$ |  | (5) |

where
$`\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}`$
represents the occurrence probability of bird species
$`\mathchar 29035`$ specific to mixture component $`\mathchar 29042`$,
$`\mathchar 28953_{\mathchar 29042}`$ is the probability weight on
component $`\mathchar 29042`$,
$`\bm{\mathchar 28953}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28953_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28953_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28953_{\mathchar 29010}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 29012}`$
and
$`\bm{\mathchar 29007}\mathchar 12349\{\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\}`$
is an $`\mathchar 29010\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28723}`$
matrix of the occurrence probabilities. For
$`\mathchar 29010\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721`$, the model assumes
independent occurrences of the different species, and as
$`\mathchar 29010`$ increases more complex dependence structures are
characterized. Unlike previous methods, such as [Bragg et al.
(2013)](#bib.bib3); [Duan et al. (2014)](#bib.bib10); [Hung et al.
(2017)](#bib.bib11), BMM flexibly captures both positive and negative
correlations among the $`\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28723}`$ bird
species.

We specify standard conjugate priors for component probabilities
$`\bm{\mathchar 28953}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28953_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28953_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28953_{\mathchar 29010}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 29012}`$
and occurrence probabilities within each component
$`\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}`$ as
follows:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\bm{\mathchar 28953}\mathchar 12824\text{Dirichlet}\delimiter 67273472\bm{\mathchar 28939}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\qquad\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12824\text{Beta}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 29039}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29026_{\mathchar 29039}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891
``` |  | (6) |

where
$`\bm{\mathchar 28939}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28939_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28939_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28939_{\mathchar 29010}\delimiter 84054785`$,
$`\mathchar 28939_{\mathchar 29042}\mathchar 12606\mathchar 28720`$,
$`\mathchar 29042\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29010`$,
is the parameter of the Dirichlet distribution, encoding our prior
beliefs about each mixture component’s weight
$`\mathchar 28953_{\mathchar 29042}`$. Commonly, $`\mathchar 29010`$ is
chosen as an upper bound on the number of components with
$`\mathchar 28939_{\mathchar 29042}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 29010`$
or some other small number to favor setting unnecessary mixture
component weights close to zero; this is sometimes referred to as an
over-fitted mixture model ([Rousseau and Mengersen, 2011](#bib.bib41);
[Van Havre et al., 2015](#bib.bib42)).

### 2.4 Dirichlet process extension

[Dunson and Xing (2009)](#bib.bib40) proposed a nonparametric Bayes
modeling approach for multivariate unordered categorical data, which is
flexible enough to accurately approximate any possible joint probability
mass function. Following their model, we assumed that each latent binary
variable
$`\bm{\mathchar 29049}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28723}}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 29012}`$
conforms to a DP mixture with countably infinite Bernoulli mixture
components. In accordance, we have mixing weights
$`\bm{\mathchar 28953}\mathchar 12349\left\{\mathchar 28953_{\mathchar 29042}\right\}_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 561}`$,
and the matrix of the occurrence probabilities in Section
[2.3](#S2.SS3 "2.3 Bernoulli mixture models ‣ 2 Materials and methods ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies")
becomes
$`\bm{\mathchar 29007}\mathchar 12349\left\{\bm{\mathchar 29039}_{\mathchar 29042}\right\}_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 561}`$,
where
$`\bm{\mathchar 29039}_{\mathchar 29042}\mathchar 12349\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28723}}\right\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 29012}`$.
We introduce $`\mathchar 28946_{\mathchar 29033}`$ and
$`\mathchar 29050_{\mathchar 29033}`$ for audio recording
$`\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12850\left\{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28721}\right\}`$,
where each $`\mathchar 28946_{\mathchar 29033}`$ designates the
parameter selected from the sample path $`\mathchar 28999`$ of the DP
that generates the corresponding latent binary variable
$`\bm{\mathchar 29049}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28723}}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 29012}`$
and $`\mathchar 29050_{\mathchar 29033}`$ indicates which mixture
component the audio recording $`\mathchar 29033`$ is assigned to.
Consequently, we can portray the infinite mixture model for
$`\bm{\mathchar 29049}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28723}}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 29012}`$
alongside the associated prior as follows:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathchar 28999\mathchar 12824\text{DP}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28941\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29000\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\quad\mathchar 28946_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12906\mathchar 28999\mathchar 12824\mathchar 28999\mathchar 24891\ \mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\quad\bm{\mathchar 29049}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12906\mathchar 28946_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12824\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\bm{\mathchar 29049}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12906\mathchar 28946_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\ \mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891
``` |  | (7) |

where
$`\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\bm{\mathchar 29049}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12906\mathchar 28946_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785`$
signifies a distribution parameterized by
$`\mathchar 28946_{\mathchar 29033}`$, and the base measure
$`\mathchar 29000`$ outlines the prior for the occurrence probabilities
of the $`\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28723}`$ bird species within each
mixture component. Specifically, the base measure $`\mathchar 29000`$
can then be expressed as:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathchar 29000\delimiter 67273472\bm{\mathchar 29039}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 4945\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28723}}\text{Beta}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 29039}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29026_{\mathchar 29039}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891`$ |  | (8) |

where
$`\bm{\mathchar 29039}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28723}}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 29012}`$
and $`\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 29039}`$ and
$`\mathchar 29026_{\mathchar 29039}`$ have the same meaning as we
indicate in Section
[2.2](#S2.SS2 "2.2 Baseline model ‣ 2 Materials and methods ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies"),
encapsulating our prior beliefs about the occurrence probabilities of
the $`\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28723}`$ bird species. For audio
recording $`\mathchar 29033`$, if
$`\mathchar 28946_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12349\bm{\mathchar 29039}_{\mathchar 29042}`$,
we can express the generative process of
$`\bm{\mathchar 29049}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28723}}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 29012}`$
in Equation
([7](#S2.E7 "In 2.4 Dirichlet process extension ‣ 2 Materials and methods ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies"))
as:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\bm{\mathchar 29049}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12906\mathchar 28946_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29040\delimiter 67273472\bm{\mathchar 29049}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12906\bm{\mathchar 29007}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29050_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29042\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 4945\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28723}}\text{Bernoulli}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314
``` |  | (9) |

Finally, we chose a
$`\text{Gamma}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29045_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29045_{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785`$
prior for the concentration parameter $`\mathchar 28941`$ in the DP
([Escobar and West, 1995](#bib.bib21); [Blei and Jordan,
2006](#bib.bib22)), where $`\mathchar 29045_{\mathchar 28721}`$ and
$`\mathchar 29045_{\mathchar 28722}`$ are hyper-parameters.

### 2.5 Hierarchical modeling of annotator expertise

In previous models, we assume the same levels of bird song
identification expertise of annotators across the
$`\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28723}`$ bird species. Since this is very
unlikely to hold in practice, we generalize Equation
([2](#S2.E2 "In 2.2 Baseline model ‣ 2 Materials and methods ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies"))
to allow experts to vary in their ability levels, both overall and in
terms of their skills with particular species, via the following
hierarchical model:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 12906\mathchar 28950_{\mathchar 28949}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28958_{\mathchar 28949}`$ | $`\displaystyle\mathchar 12824\mathcal{\mathchar 29006}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28950_{\mathchar 28949}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28958_{\mathchar 28949}^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\quad\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12906\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28958_{\mathchar 28949}^{\mathchar 8707}\mathchar 12824\mathcal{\mathchar 29006}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28958_{\mathchar 28949}^{\mathchar 8707}\right\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 28722}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891`$ |  | (10) |
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 12906\mathchar 28950_{\mathchar 28960}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28958_{\mathchar 28960}`$ | $`\displaystyle\mathchar 12824\mathcal{\mathchar 29006}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28950_{\mathchar 28960}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28958_{\mathchar 28960}^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\quad\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12906\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28958_{\mathchar 28960}^{\mathchar 8707}\mathchar 12824\mathcal{\mathchar 29006}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28958_{\mathchar 28960}^{\mathchar 8707}\right\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 28722}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891`$ |  |  |

where
$`\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29034}\right\delimiter 84054785`$
have different meanings compared to their usage in Base and DP-BMM and
represent annotator $`\mathchar 29034`$’s overall bird identification
expertise,
$`\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\right\delimiter 84054785`$
represent annotator $`\mathchar 29034`$’s identification expertise for
species $`\mathchar 29035`$, and
$`\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28958_{\mathchar 28949}^{\mathchar 8707}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28958_{\mathchar 28960}^{\mathchar 8707}\delimiter 84054785`$
control the variability of annotators’ identification expertise across
different species. Incorporating this hierarchical structure in Equation
([10](#S2.E10 "In 2.5 Hierarchical modeling of annotator expertise ‣ 2 Materials and methods ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies")),
the measurement error process for each bird expert is as follows:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\text{Pr}\delimiter 67273472\mathcal{\mathchar 29012}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28955\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\qquad\text{Pr}\delimiter 67273472\mathcal{\mathchar 29012}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28955\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891
``` |  | (11) |

for
$`\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 29011}_{\mathchar 29034}`$,
where
$`\mathchar 28955\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29050\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8235\exp\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29050\delimiter 84054785}}`$
is the logistic transformation function. For
$`\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 29011}_{\mathchar 29034}`$,
a more compact form of Equation
([11](#S2.E11 "In 2.5 Hierarchical modeling of annotator expertise ‣ 2 Materials and methods ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies"))
is:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathcal{\mathchar 29012}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12824\text{Bernoulli}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28955\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}}\mathchar 28955\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\ \mathchar 29034\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 314`$ |  | (12) |

If annotator $`\mathchar 29034`$ does not have expertise in species
$`\mathchar 29035`$ then we do not include any data from that annotator
on that species in our analysis.

### 2.6 Posterior computation

For the models summarized in Table
[1](#S2.T1 "Table 1 ‣ 2 Materials and methods ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies"),
we developed corresponding easy to implement Gibbs samplers. To improve
efficiency in posterior sampling for our DP mixture models, we
marginalized out
$`\bm{\mathchar 29039}_{\mathchar 29042}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28723}}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 29012}`$
and $`\mathchar 28953_{\mathchar 29042}`$ for each mixture component,
and derive a collapsed Gibbs sampler ([Liu, 1994](#bib.bib23)), which
samples the assignments of the $`\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28721}`$
audio recordings. For models taking the variability of annotators’ sound
identification expertise across species into consideration, we adopted
the Pólya-Gamma (PG) augmentation technique widely used in logistic
regression-type models ([Polson et al., 2013](#bib.bib19)). Details are
provided in Appendix 1.

### 2.7 Simulation setup

We assessed the proposed framework through simulation studies under a
range of data-generating mechanisms, sparsity levels, and prior
settings. In all experiments, we generated
$`\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720`$ audio
recordings, $`\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720`$ annotators, and
$`\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28725`$ bird species.

For posterior inference, we ran three chains of
$`\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720`$
iterations each for Base and DP-BMM. We discarded the first
$`\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720`$ draws
in each chain as burn-in, and collected sufficient samples to ensure a
minimum of $`\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720`$ effective
sample size (ESS) for each parameter. For the more complex
Base-Hierarchical and DP-BMM-Hierarchical models, to achieve the minimum
ESS requirement, each chain was run for
$`\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720`$
iterations, with the initial
$`\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720`$ draws
discarded as burn-in. In addition to estimating ESS values for different
parameters, we assessed MCMC convergence and mixing via Gelman–Rubin
statistics, which were below
$`\mathchar 28721\mathchar 314\mathchar 28721`$ for all parameters in
all experiments.

We considered scenarios that vary according to whether the occurrences
of different species are independent or correlated, whether annotators’
expertise varies across species, the average number of annotations per
recording ($`\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28728`$,
$`\mathchar 28721\mathchar 314\mathchar 28726`$,
$`\mathchar 28722\mathchar 314\mathchar 28724`$,
$`\mathchar 28723\mathchar 314\mathchar 28722`$,
$`\mathchar 28724\mathchar 314\mathchar 28720`$), and the prior
specification used in the analysis. We used the default prior (see
Section
[2.8](#S2.SS8 "2.8 Application-specific prior specification and evaluation setup ‣ 2 Materials and methods ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies"))
and also performed a sensitivity analysis on the choice of priors.

When occurrences of species are independent, we drew
$`\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12824\text{Beta}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28728\delimiter 84054785`$,
$`\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28725`$,
and then generated the indicators
$`\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12824\text{Bernoulli}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 84054785`$,
$`\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720`$,
$`\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28725`$,
for all species in each recording. When occurrences of species are
correlated, we drew the indicators
$`\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}`$ for
the first $`\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28725`$ species as described above,
and then directly set
$`\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28725}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}`$,
$`\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720`$,
$`\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720`$.
This represents an extreme scenario in which species $`\mathchar 29035`$
and species
$`\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28725`$ always
co-occur.

We generated three types of annotators: random
($`\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\%`$), normal
($`\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28720\%`$), and excellent
($`\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720\%`$). The average across-species TPRs
for individuals in these three groups were drawn independently from
$`\text{Uniform}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28726\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28720\delimiter 84054785`$,
$`\text{Uniform}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28725\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28728\mathchar 28725\delimiter 84054785`$,
and
$`\text{Uniform}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28725\delimiter 84054785`$,
respectively. As birders tend not to record a bird species as being
present unless they are almost certain, FPRs tend to be very low; hence,
we generated average across-species FPRs for all individuals
independently from
$`\text{Uniform}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785`$.
For cases in which expertise does not vary across species, we generated
$`\mathcal{\mathchar 29012}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}`$
from
$`\text{Bernoulli}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034}^{\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}}\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29034}^{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}}\right\delimiter 84054785`$.
For cases in which expertise does vary, we characterized this
variability and generated annotators’ species-specific TPRs
$`\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}`$ and
FPRs
$`\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}`$, as
well as annotations
$`\mathcal{\mathchar 29012}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}`$,
through Equations
([10](#S2.E10 "In 2.5 Hierarchical modeling of annotator expertise ‣ 2 Materials and methods ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies"))
–
([12](#S2.E12 "In 2.5 Hierarchical modeling of annotator expertise ‣ 2 Materials and methods ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies")),
where
$`\mathchar 28958_{\mathchar 28949}^{\mathchar 8707}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722`$
and
$`\mathchar 28958_{\mathchar 28960}^{\mathchar 8707}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721`$.

Since the goal of our simulation studies was to assess the performance
of our approach in realistic settings, we started with a default prior
as in the real data analysis and then conducted a sensitivity analysis.
We considered different combinations of priors for the occurrence
probabilities of species and annotators’ TPRs. Our default prior for the
occurrence probability was
$`\text{Beta}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28722\mathchar 8706\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28722\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8706\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\delimiter 84054785`$.
Then, we varied the prior mean within the plausible set
$`\{\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28725\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28725\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28723\mathchar 28720\}`$.
Our default prior for annotators’ $`\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034}`$
in Base and DP-BMM was
$`\text{Beta}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28728\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8706\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28728\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8706\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720\delimiter 84054785`$;
we varied the prior mean within
$`\{\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28725\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28728\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28728\mathchar 28724\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28728\mathchar 28727\}`$.
For Base-Hierarchical and DP-BMM-Hierarchical, the default prior for
$`\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034}`$ was
$`\mathcal{\mathchar 29006}\delimiter 67273472\log\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28728\mathchar 28721\delimiter 68408078\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28728\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28728^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785`$;
the other choices of priors for $`\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034}`$
were
$`\mathcal{\mathchar 29006}\delimiter 67273472\log\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28725\delimiter 68408078\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28725\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28723^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785`$,
$`\mathcal{\mathchar 29006}\delimiter 67273472\log\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28728\delimiter 68408078\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28728\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28725^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785`$,
$`\mathcal{\mathchar 29006}\delimiter 67273472\log\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28728\mathchar 28724\delimiter 68408078\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28728\mathchar 28724\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28726\mathchar 28723^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785`$,
and
$`\mathcal{\mathchar 29006}\delimiter 67273472\log\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28728\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28725\delimiter 68408078\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28728\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28725\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28722^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785`$.
As for annotators’ FPRs, we used
$`\text{Beta}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28725\mathchar 8706\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28725\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8706\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\delimiter 84054785`$
as the prior in Base and DP-BMM, and used
$`\mathcal{\mathchar 29006}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28725\delimiter 68408078\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28725\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28724\mathchar 28721^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785`$
as the prior in Base-Hierarchical and DP-BMM-Hierarchical. For DP-BMM
and DP-BMM-Hierarchical, we placed a
$`\text{Gamma}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28725\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28725\delimiter 84054785`$
prior on concentration parameter $`\mathchar 28941`$; for
Base-Hierarchical and DP-BMM-Hierarchical, we employed an empirical
Bayes approach to estimate the hyperparameters for annotators’
species-specific identification expertise. Specifically, we maximized
the likelihood with respect to
$`\mathchar 28958_{\mathchar 28949}^{\mathchar 8707}`$ and
$`\mathchar 28958_{\mathchar 28960}^{\mathchar 8707}`$, and updated them
as follows:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28958_{\mathchar 28949}^{\mathchar 8707}\right\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12349{{\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28725}\mathbb{\mathchar 29001}\{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12850\mathchar 29036_{\mathchar 29034}\}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 28722}\over\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28725}\mathbb{\mathchar 29001}\{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12850\mathchar 29036_{\mathchar 29034}\}}}\mathchar 24891\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28958_{\mathchar 28960}^{\mathchar 8707}\right\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12349{{\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28725}\mathbb{\mathchar 29001}\{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12850\mathchar 29036_{\mathchar 29034}\}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29034}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 28722}\over\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28725}\mathbb{\mathchar 29001}\{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12850\mathchar 29036_{\mathchar 29034}\}}}\mathchar 24891

### 2.8 Application-specific prior specification and evaluation setup

For the real-data analysis, we used bird species annotation data from
the Finnish Kerttu project introduced in Section
[2.1](#S2.SS1 "2.1 Motivating application and problem formulation ‣ 2 Materials and methods ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies"),
available at
[https://zenodo.org/record/7030863#.Y6GgtIRBwuU](https://zenodo.org/record/7030863#.Y6GgtIRBwuU).
The data are highly sparse, so careful prior elicitation is important.

Similar to the simulation studies, we ran three chains of
$`\mathchar 28723\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720`$
iterations each for Base and DP-BMM. We discarded the first
$`\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720`$ draws
in each chain as burn-in. For Base-Hierarchical and DP-BMM-Hierarchical,
each chain was run for
$`\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720`$
iterations, with the initial
$`\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720`$ draws
discarded as burn-in for both models. For the stickiest
$`\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}`$’s in
both Base-Hierarchical and DP-BMM-Hierarchical, we obtained
approximately $`\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720`$ ESS, but such low ESS
values were only observed for a small fraction of the
$`\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}`$’s.
The overwhelming majority of parameters had ESS over
$`\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720`$. Further diagnostics
of MCMC convergence and mixing are provided in Appendix
[C](#A3 "Appendix C MCMC diagnostics for application to species annotation data ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies").

We first specified priors for Base and DP-BMM, assuming uniform
identification expertise across species. Based on the domain knowledge
described in Section
[2.1](#S2.SS1 "2.1 Motivating application and problem formulation ‣ 2 Materials and methods ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies"),
we expect (i) the average TPR among our annotators to be
$`\mathchar 12824\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28729`$, (ii)
nearly $`\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28725\%`$ of annotators achieve TPRs
exceeding $`\mathchar 28728\mathchar 28725\%`$, and (iii) the majority
exhibit FPRs around
$`\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28725`$.
Taking into consideration factors such as multiple bird species
vocalizing simultaneously and potential background noise, we set the
hyperparameters for $`\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034}`$ and
$`\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29034}`$ to
$`\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 28949}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28724\mathchar 28725`$,
$`\mathchar 29026_{\mathchar 28949}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28725`$,
$`\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 28960}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28725`$, and
$`\mathchar 29026_{\mathchar 28960}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28725`$,
resulting in the equal-tailed $`\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28725\%`$ prior
credible interval for $`\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034}`$ to be
$`\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28728\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28724\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28726\mathchar 28726\delimiter 84054785`$
and for $`\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29034}`$ to be
$`\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28726\mathchar 28723\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28722\delimiter 84054785`$.
For the bird species occurrence probabilities, we know that there are
usually only two or three species present in a recording. If we ignore
correlations among species and consider the occurrence of one species as
a Bernoulli trial, the prior for $`\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29035}`$
should satisfy
$`\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 29039}\delimiter 68408078\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 29039}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29026_{\mathchar 29039}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12825\mathchar 28722\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28723}`$,
where
$`\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28723}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28727`$
in our problem. Moreover, as $`\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28725\%`$ of
recordings contain at most $`\mathchar 28724`$ or $`\mathchar 28725`$
species, the hyperparameters for $`\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29035}`$
in Base and
$`\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}`$ in
DP-BMM are chosen to be
$`\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 29039}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722`$ and
$`\mathchar 29026_{\mathchar 29039}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28728`$,
leading to equal-tailed $`\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28725\%`$ prior
credible intervals for both $`\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29035}`$ and
$`\mathchar 29039_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29035}`$ of
$`\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28724\mathchar 28726\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28720\delimiter 84054785`$.

We next specified priors for Base-Hierarchical and DP-BMM-Hierarchical,
incorporating the hierarchical structure of annotators’ identification
expertise. For the overall expertise of annotator $`\mathchar 29034`$,
we set the hyperparameters as
$`\mathchar 28950_{\mathchar 28949}\mathchar 12349\log\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28729\delimiter 68408078\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28729\delimiter 84054785\right\delimiter 84054785`$,
$`\mathchar 28958_{\mathchar 28949}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28724\mathchar 28728`$,
$`\mathchar 28950_{\mathchar 28960}\mathchar 12349\log\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28725\delimiter 68408078\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28725\delimiter 84054785\right\delimiter 84054785`$,
and
$`\mathchar 28958_{\mathchar 28960}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28724\mathchar 28725`$.
These result in equal-tailed $`\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28725\%`$ prior
credible intervals for
$`\mathchar 28955\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29034}\delimiter 84054785`$
of
$`\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28728\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28729\delimiter 84054785`$
and for
$`\mathchar 28955\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28960_{\mathchar 29034}\delimiter 84054785`$
of
$`\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28727\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720\delimiter 84054785`$.
Similar to the simulation studies, we employed an empirical Bayes
approach to estimate
$`\mathchar 28958_{\mathchar 28949}^{\mathchar 8707}`$ and
$`\mathchar 28958_{\mathchar 28960}^{\mathchar 8707}`$.

For evaluation of species identification, based on the results of Base,
we identified $`\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28725`$
recordings with the highest levels of uncertainty, which potentially
contain bird species that are difficult to identify. We then asked an
experienced and reliable ornithologist to provide annotations for these
recordings. These annotations were used as the gold standard for
evaluating recovery of
$`\{\bm{\mathchar 29049}_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\bm{\mathchar 29049}_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\bm{\mathchar 29049}_{\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 28721}}\}`$
from noisy $`\mathcal{\mathchar 29012}`$.

## 3 Results

In this Section
[3](#S3 "3 Results ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies"),
we test the performance of several methods within our modeling framework
on simulated data (Section
[3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Simulation results ‣ 3 Results ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies"))
and a dataset of Finnish bird vocalizations which has been annotated by
bird experts through a crowdsourcing project ([Lehikoinen et al.,
2023](#bib.bib30)) (Section
[3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Results for bird species annotations ‣ 3 Results ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies")).
We compare our methods to Majority Vote approach (MV), which was
originally used for annotation aggregation by [Lehikoinen et al.
(2023)](#bib.bib30).

### 3.1 Simulation results

We present results under the simulation settings described in Section
[2.7](#S2.SS7 "2.7 Simulation setup ‣ 2 Materials and methods ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies").
We evaluate the proposed framework in terms of species identification
and estimation of annotators’ bird-song identification expertise.

#### 3.1.1 Species identification

We assess the performance of different annotation aggregation methods
under different generative mechanisms in terms of the Area under the
Curve (AUC). Under the default priors for annotators’ TPRs and the
occurrence probabilities, performance is summarized in Table
[2](#S3.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 3.1.1 Species identification ‣ 3.1 Simulation results ‣ 3 Results ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies")
for the four scenarios – Scenario 1: independent species and no
variability of annotators’ identification expertise, Scenario 2:
correlated species and no variability, Scenario 3: independent species
and variability and Scenario 4: correlated species and variability.
Sensitivity analyses are provided in Appendix
[B.1](#A2.SS1 "B.1 Species identification ‣ Appendix B Additional results for simulation studies ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies").

Table 2: AUCs of different methods under four different scenarios with
default priors. \#Anns represents the number of annotations for each
recording.

Generally speaking, performance of all methods in terms of AUCs improves
significantly with an increasing number of annotations per recording for
all scenarios. All of our models consistently outperform MV across
scenarios, which demonstrates the necessity of taking annotators’
identification expertise into account when aggregating annotations. As
expected, DP-BMM/DP-BMM-Hierarchical perform slightly better than
Base/Base-Hierarchical when the occurrences of species are correlated.
When the number of annotations per recording is low, the performance gap
is even larger, suggesting that combining BMMs with measurement error
with multiple annotators is effective in our settings. In comparing
Base/DP-BMM and Base-Hierarchical/DP-BMM-Hierarchical, the latter
performs much better in the presence of variability across species in
annotator expertise. Based on the sensitivity analyses in Appendix
[B.1](#A2.SS1 "B.1 Species identification ‣ Appendix B Additional results for simulation studies ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies"),
we find the performance of the models doesn’t change significantly under
reasonable changes to the prior.

#### 3.1.2 Assessment of species identification expertise

In addition to species identification, we evaluate the accuracy of the
estimated annotators’ TPRs for methods except MV under different
generative mechanisms in terms of coverages of
$`\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28725\%`$ credible intervals (CIs) and mean
squared errors (MSEs). As shown in Section
[3.1.1](#S3.SS1.SSS1 "3.1.1 Species identification ‣ 3.1 Simulation results ‣ 3 Results ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies"),
we summarize the coverages for annotators’ TPRs in Table
[3](#S3.T3 "Table 3 ‣ 3.1.2 Assessment of species identification expertise ‣ 3.1 Simulation results ‣ 3 Results ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies")
and the MSEs in Table
[4](#S3.T4 "Table 4 ‣ 3.1.2 Assessment of species identification expertise ‣ 3.1 Simulation results ‣ 3 Results ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies")
under default priors. Sensitivity analyses are provided in Appendix
[B.2](#A2.SS2 "B.2 Assessment of sound identification expertise ‣ Appendix B Additional results for simulation studies ‣ Bayesian modeling of multi-species labeling errors in ecological studies").
````
